#290. Surface Laptop Ultra review: The most powerful Surface yet, but it still lags behind Apple

It’s a beautiful flagship device for NVIDIA’s RTX Spark.

With a name like Surface Laptop Ultra, Microsoft's latest notebook better be impressive. It's one of the flagship devices for NVIDIA's new RTX Spark platform, making it the most powerful Surface device yet. It can be equipped with up to 128GB of unified memory, which is ideal for running local AI models. And in just about every other respect, from its super-bright 15-inch screen to its sleek case, the Surface Laptop Ultra screams quality. So sure, the name fits.

But with its high $2,599 starting price, which easily escalates if you want to go beyond its stock 24GB of RAM, the Surface Laptop Ultra is really only meant for the most demanding users. To be fair, though, the same is true of all the RTX Spark laptops, including the excellent ASUS ProArt P16 we recently reviewed. Even Apple is pushing the limits of laptop pricing with its new touchscreen MacBook Pros with PROLED screens, which start at $2.899.

Like some sort of cosmic capitalist joke, PC makers have inexplicably decided to release pricier computers, even though we're already suffering from AI-driven memory price hikes. I'm sure the Surface Laptop Ultra's high price won't stop AI enthusiasts who've been eagerly awaiting capable Windows systems from buying one, but most gamers and content creators would likely be better off saving $1,000 and buying a capable gaming laptop instead.

Much like the standard Surface Laptop, the Ultra variant doesn't have any of the bells and whistles from Microsoft's convertible Surfaces. It just looks like a sleek, all-metal business laptop. Pop it open and you'll notice that its 15-inch screen has a tall 3:2 aspect ratio instead of the typical widescreen configuration. And if you peer along the bottom of the case, you'll see a slightly segmented base (reminiscent of the Surface Laptop Studio), which makes a bit of room for cooling vents and downward-firing speakers.

While there's a lot to admire about Microsoft's design prowess, the biggest selling point of the Surface Laptop Ultra is NVIDIA's RTX Spark chip. It's an Arm-based system-on-a-chip that can be equipped with up to 20 CPU cores and 6,144 GPU cores. Take note: You'll need to shell out $3,700 to get the most powerful RTX Spark hardware here, but at least that ships with 32GB of RAM. The base $2,600 Surface Laptop Ultra has 18 CPU cores and 5,120 GPU cores.

Taking a cue from Apple Silicon and Qualcomm's recent Snapdragon X chips, the RTX Spark also features unified memory, which is accessible to both the CPU and GPU. That gives it even more memory bandwidth than a typical Intel or AMD-based Windows PC, which have their RAM, CPU and dedicated GPUs spread out across their motherboards. By having direct access to tons of RAM, RTX Spark hardware can also house large AI models locally, which is a major reason why both Microsoft and NVIDIA are pitching it as a major evolution for PCs.

Thanks to its NVIDIA GPU cores, the RTX Spark chip also delivers gaming performance similar to an RTX 5070 laptop GPU. That makes it ideal for 1440p gaming, and you can probably also get decent performance in 4K and other higher resolutions thanks to NVIDIA's DLSS upscaling and frame generation. The Surface Laptop Ultra starts at a much higher price than most RTX 5070 gaming notebooks, though, so it's not something you'd want to buy for pure gaming.

The RTX Spark's Arm CPU is both a blessing and a curse: It technically can perform more efficiently than Intel and AMD's x86-64 chips, but it also requires emulating older Windows software and drivers. That opens the door to unforeseen bugs and issues, especially for niche apps and minimally-supported third-party drivers. While this was a major problem when Microsoft first started dabbling with Windows on Arm (think back to the original Surface with Windows RT or the Surface Pro X), the company's emulation for x86-64 software has dramatically improved. Kernel-level anti-cheat software for multiplayer games initially didn't work on the first Snapdragon X Windows systems, but both Microsoft and NVIDIA have worked on resolving that. Now, you can easily play Fortnite Arm-based Windows systems.

Microsoft initially touted the Surface Laptop Ultra's 15-inch screen as the brightest laptop display on the market, thanks to its eye-searing 2,000 nits of HDR brightness. Apple's new MacBook Pro with PROLED Display stole that thunder a bit, though: Its screen can reach the same brightness, and its OLED screen delivers deeper blacks and higher contrast. The Surface Laptop Ultra's mini-LED display is still plenty impressive though, especially while watching films in HDR with rich colors, or playing games that truly push its brightness and HDR capabilities.

The Surface Laptop Ultra also features a keyboard that's similar to the Surface Laptop's, which has always felt great to type on thanks to its deep key depth and satisfying feedback. Its large trackpad also supports newer haptic feedback in Windows — for example, you can feel a slight nudge as you move apps along the edges of the screen to lock them into place. We've seen similar haptic feedback in mice like Logitech's MX Master 4, and while it's not exactly groundbreaking, I find it to be a nifty addition to the aging Windows interface.

Strangely enough, one of the most interesting things about the Surface Laptop Ultra is its magnetic USB-C charging cable. It allows the notebook's charging cable to snap into place on one USB-C port, and similar to Apple's MagSafe cable, it also lets the cable easily slide out if someone trips on it. It's a useful replacement for the proprietary Surface charging connection, since it allows Microsoft to keep a standard USB-C port on the Laptop Ultra for charging, which will also work seamlessly with every other USB-C charging cable. The magnetic USB-C charging cable is Microsoft's invention, but the company says it could bring it to partner laptops if they're interested.

The Surface Laptop Pro's ports mostly match the MacBook Pro's, with three USB-C ports, HDMI, SD card slot and a headphone jack. But, crucially, there's also a USB Type-A connection for older devices. I could see that being very useful for some Windows creatives.

The Surface Laptop is a beautiful beast. While every RTX Spark laptop will be similarly speedy, Microsoft has gone to great effort to make the Surface Laptop Ultra stand out from every other Windows notebook. Its aluminum case feels both as solid as a rock, and as smooth as a piece of fine jewelry. It also rejects fingerprints so it always looks pristine — something the ASUS ProArt P16 completely failed at. I wish the Surface Laptop Ultra was a bit lighter, though: At 4.4 pounds, it feels more like a brick than I'd expect for a premium notebook in 2026. Dell's XPS 16 weighs 3.65 pounds, and Apple's new 16-inch PROLED MacBook Pro comes in at 4 pounds.

In benchmarks, the RTX Spark chip excelled at multi-threaded tests, like Cinebench 2024, where it scored several hundred points higher than the ASUS ProArt PX13, which was powered by AMD's Ryzen AI Max+ 395 chip. NVIDIA still lags behind Apple, though. The new PROLED MacBook Pro, which was powered by an M5 Pro chip, scored 2,188 points in that same test, whereas the Surface Laptop Ultra came in at 1,553 points. Apple also has the lead in single-threaded performance: The M5 Pro scored 192 points to the Surface's 120. Apple's newer M6 chip in the 14-inch MacBook Pro does even better, reaching 210 points.

NVIDIA's big draw is that its chip can power Windows and Linux, while you'll need to become a macOS convert to use Apple's hardware. While doing so isn't tough these days, I've talked to AI researchers who rely on Windows apps to get their work done. Moving to a Mac would just be a pain for them.

The Surface Laptop Ultra scored 1,000 points lower than the ASUS ProArt P16 in the PCMark 10 Applications test, which is surprising since they're both using the same RTX Spark chip. It could be a difference in RAM: The Surface Laptop we tested had 32GB of RAM, while the ASUS was stuffed to the brim with 128GB. But in real-world usage, that extra RAM is only useful if you want to run large AI models on your system, it doesn't directly mean faster performance. And personally, I'll take Microsoft's refined build quality over slightly better benchmark speeds any day.

With 32GB of RAM, our review unit couldn't run the massive AI models that fit into the ASUS P16, but I was still able to get OpenClaw running with Qwen3.6 35B-A38. It was quick to fetch me updates on recent news, as well as transcribe audio from a podcast. I even had it generate an audio podcast from a written review — sacrilege for a podcaster, I know — and it managed to do so in a few minutes. I don't personally use agentic AI models, but with a bit more RAM, I could see the Surface Laptop Ultra being a fitting home for one.

Given its GPU prowess, I wasn't surprised to see the Surface Laptop Ultra reach 87 fps in Cyberpunk 2077 while playing in 1440p with maxed-out graphics settings, 4x frame generation and path tracing enabled. That would be my ideal way to play that game on a 15-inch screen. I could even hit 117 fps by pushing the frame generation to 6x, but that leads to occasional graphical weirdness. I was also able to crank up the graphics settings in Star Wars: Galactic Racer and Gears of War: E-Day while keeping a decently high frame rate.

While I would have liked to see more than a 120Hz refresh rate on the Surface Laptop Ultra, that's still fast enough to make most games look pretty smooth. (There's also the additional benefit of making scrolling around Windows look much smoother.) And I can't praise Microsoft's screen technology enough. At times, I preferred playing Gears of War on the Surface Laptop Ultra instead of my 32-inch 4K gaming monitor, simply because it looked so much brighter.

As for battery life, the Surface Laptop Ultra lasted for 13 hours and 25 minutes in the PCMark 10 battery benchmark. That's two and a half hours longer than the ASUS ProArt P16 and 90 minutes more than the XPS 16. During the workday, I used the system for around seven and a half hours before it conked out, handling a variety of video calls, writing and web browsing across dozens of tabs.

If you're a diehard Windows user (yes, they exist) who's really into AI, and you want one of the most premium laptops around, the Surface Laptop Ultra is a solid choice. But just be aware that you'll have to pay out the nose to enjoy it. Our review unit, which was equipped with 32GB of RAM, 1TB of storage and the full-powered RTX Spark chip, sells for $3,700. Bumping up to 64GB of RAM would run you $4,300, and it jumps to $5,900 with 128GB of RAM. All of a sudden, the $2,600 base price doesn't seem so bad.

It's strange that the Surface Laptop Ultra maxes out at 2TB of storage, though. Other premium systems have offered up to 4TB for years, shouldn't the "Ultra" Surface device at least match that?

If you have deep pockets and want the most for your money, though, Apple's MacBook Pros are still a better choice when it comes to pure power. NVIDIA's first-gen RTX Spark chip just can't beat the M5 Pro, and it's certainly no match for even more capable M5 Max and Ultra chips. Sure, they'll cost a ton, but you can also get a 16-inch MacBook Pro PROLED that weighs just four pounds and is stuffed with 128GB of RAM. If you're already spending close to $10,000 on a laptop, why not get the best one possible?

Microsoft has finally managed to make a powerful Surface device with modern hardware, something it failed to do with the previous Surface Book and Laptop Studio systems. The Surface Laptop Ultra's sheer refinement is a sign of just how far Microsoft has come. It sure is expensive, though. And even with its high price tag, it still lags behind Apple's hardware when it comes to raw performance.

#287. Why Apple's HomeView relies so heavily on your iPhone

"There's no product in the world better designed to be your intelligent personal hub than iPhone."

That's the statement newly minted Apple CEO John Ternus made during his opening remarks at his first iPhone event this year. It should be no surprise then, that the device holding so much of your data would play a vital role with a separate "intelligent personal hub" that stays inside your home.

This week marks the arrival of the HomeView, Apple's long-rumored smart display that was announced last month. As I explained in detail in my review, it's basically a combination of several gadgets in one. It's both a visual interface and a smart speaker that is always listening for your Siri queries. It's a smart home hub for a range of connected devices. It's a sous chef in the kitchen, and it's a FaceTime station when you need to catch up with friends and family. Heck, it's even a digital photo frame.

The HomeView doesn't run apps though. It performs all those functions, and more, by mirroring the apps that you have on your iPhone or iPad. Instead of allowing you to download software from the App Store, the HomeView's system works like CarPlay does in your car. It shows you the compatible apps you've already installed on your phone or tablet, as well as Apple's core suite, including Calendar, Messages, Reminders and Weather.

This is just one of the reasons an iPhone (or iPad) needs to be close by for the HomeView to work.

"When we design home products, we always keep three things in mind," Cecilia Dantas, a member of Apple's Home product marketing team, told me. "They have to be intelligent, personal and private."

"Everything that feels natural on a phone is now available in a subtle, glanceable device that gives you what you need in the moment," VP of hardware engineering Matthew Costello said. "It recognizes you and shows you your information, but when you step away, it's gone."

Costello further explained that the HomeView offers the best combination of personal information with the convenience of a dedicated communal device. Since the iPhone is a user's hub for their personal content — and really, their life — it only made sense to "extend and link that intelligence" into products like the HomeView.

If the HomeView doesn't recognize a user, it will only allow them to perform basic tasks. And even if you are a recognized user, you'll need to have your recently unlocked iPhone or iPad nearby for the smart display to show things like your Messages or Calendar.

By tying what shows up for you on the HomeView to your phone, your data is displayed while you're interacting with the device and disappears with you when you do. As Costello explained, it's not "posted on or housed on the device in any way."

The heavy reliance on an iPhone or an iPad has additional benefits. Like you can on a HomePod, the HomeView allows you to hand off music, FaceTime calls and more. One of the device's best features is recipe integration in Safari that makes cooking easier. It's also powered by the hand off action, so you need that other Apple device to kickstart the process. But Dantas explained that Apple doesn't see reliance on your phone or tablet as a hindrance.

"It's not just dependency, it's also part of this ecosystem," she said. The HomeView's similarity to your phone doesn't just enable familiar apps, but also a familiar method of interaction.

Speaking of interaction, there's a reason why there aren't any buttons on the HomeView. Apple intentionally built it like this. I found it odd, considering the device is a bit of a hybrid of an iPad (touchscreen), an Apple Watch (square shape) and a HomePod (speaker), two of which have physical buttons. But Costello said touch input was always meant to be secondary.

"We always envisioned it as something that was voice first, then touch second," he explained. "As we [designed] the product, that's how we thought about it, so that eliminated buttons and physical controls."

For Dantas, one of the main challenges during the product development phase was restraint. The process of bringing a new product and new software to life was obviously a tall task, but the engineering team also had to determine which parts of Apple's existing devices and services to include and which ones to leave behind.

"We've had home products for a while, and we built on that foundation," she said. "I think it was really, really challenging to go through and decide what do we bring and what do we not bring [to the HomeView]. That was very intentional to deliver this experience that's powerful and that's simple."

#286. Windows 0-day dropped by disgruntled researcher hands hackers System rights

“SignDrop” exploit lets local users gain all-powerful System rights.

Nightmare Eclipse, the pseudonym of a researcher with an ongoing, very public beef with Microsoft, has dropped yet another Windows zero-day.

On Tuesday, Nightmare Eclipse disclosed SignDrop, a vulnerability that gives local users full System rights on some versions of Windows. The vulnerability resides in the Common Log File System, which Microsoft describes as a “general-purpose logging service used by software clients running in user-mode or kernel-mode.” By forcing a privileged process such as Windows Defender to open a crafted CLFS container, an attacker with ordinary user rights can abuse the privileged file creation to elevate privileges to System.

The attack exploits the interaction of CLFS, Windows Defender, and Windows file permissions. The exploit uses Windows Defender to open a log file created by a standard user by calling ServerMpUpdateEngineSignature, a legitimate Defender function for processing antivirus signature updates.

By manipulating Windows Object Manager symbolic links, the attacker causes Defender to open the CLFS container in the security context of the antimalware engine. If an associated authentication file is missing, CLFS creates it using Defender’s System privileges. Because CLFS assigns the newly created file the Discretionary Access Control List of the original file, the attacker may gain access to a file created with System privileges, allowing the file-creation operation to be abused as part of a privilege-escalation attack.

Ironically, the vulnerability stems from a security measure Microsoft introduced in October 2025 to prevent attackers from exploiting other vulnerabilities in CLFS.

“If everything is chained correctly, funnily this legit security mitigation can be used against itself and allow an elevation [of] privileges,” Nightmare Eclipse wrote. “Everything is done properly by Microsoft here but the logic allows abuse.”

What makes it funnier is the fact that it can be used against any antimalware engine to bypass their self defense (or tamper protection) mechanism as you can trigger arbitrary file creation in the context of their own antimalware process so from the perspective of their kernel drivers, the file creation request is coming from their own legit process so it passes, this makes this vulnerability unique as it can be used to turn off any antivirus/EDR.

Nightmare Eclipse said the exploit worked against Windows 11 26H2, Windows 11 26H2 Experimental, and Windows Server 2025 Experimental. People can see if their systems are vulnerable by checking the CLFS authentication mode. If it’s in learning mode or enforcement mode, the system is vulnerable, and the PoC supplied in the post should spawn a SYSTEM shell. Nightmare Eclipse has published numerous other Windows zero-days in recent months.

Researcher Will Dormann said in an interview that he has been able to reproduce the exploit and that it works as described, but not on all versions of Windows 11. But there’s a catch, he said.

“The requirements are important in that it seems to require Windows 26H2, and one that has existed for more than 90 days, it seems,” he explained. “That is, you can’t just take a new 26H2 system and install updates and have it work.”

For a system to be vulnerable, Dormann said, the HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\CLFS\Authentication\Mode value must be 0 or 1.” Of the many machines Dormann uses, only a single one met this requirement.

Microsoft does staged rollouts of Windows updates, such as 26H2, so it’s not clear how many people have it. You can check by running the “winver” command. In a statement sent after this post went live, Microsoft said it was investigating the writeup and had no evidence the vulnerability was being exploited in the wild. The company also said Defender blocks potential exploitation attempts.

This story was updated to add Microsoft’s comment.

#284. CEOs are tired of being held responsible for gun regulation

Americans have grown used to corporate executives treading the well-worn paths of the Northeast corridor to convene alongside elected officials in Washington, DC, and discuss geopolitics, policy and all that’s in-between.

In 2017, major CEOs from across the country came together to oppose North Carolina’s transgender bathroom law. In 2019, they called abortion bans “bad for business.”

After the deadly attack on the US Capitol on January 6, 2021, many of corporate America’s biggest names denounced the rioters and pledged to halt their political giving.

Recently, more than 1,000 companies promised to voluntarily curtail their operations in Russia in protest of Moscow’s war on Ukraine.

Dick’s Sporting Goods stopped selling semi-automatic, assault-style rifles at stores and Citigroup put new restrictions on gun sales by business customers after the mass shooting at a high school in Parkland, Florida, in 2018.

A year later, after mass shootings at a Walmart in El Paso, Texas, and a nightclub in Dayton, Ohio, Walmart ended handgun ammunition sales.

Corporate leadership has long been vocal on the issue of gun control – in 2019 and again this past summer nearly 150 major companies – including Lululemon, Lyft, Bain Capital, Bloomberg LP, Permanente Medical Group and Unilever – called gun violence a “public health crisis” and demanded that the US Senate pass legislation to address it.

That’s why corporate America’s silence in the wake of the latest mass shooting at a school in Nashville is so jarring. The United States has come to rely on the increasing power of large corporations as political advocates.

But Yale professor Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, a vocal advocate of corporate social responsibility who has a direct line to major CEOs around the globe, said that top executives are forlorn. Their previous efforts haven’t done much to push the needle on gun control legislation and without more backing, they don’t know what else they can do at the moment, he said.

Before the Bell spoke with Sonnenfeld, who runs Yale School of Management’s Chief Executive Leadership Institute, a nonprofit educational and research institute focused on CEO leadership and corporate governance.

This interview has been edited for clarity and length.

Before the Bell: CEOs have been quiet about gun reform since the latest mass school shooting in Nashville, have you heard anything about plans to speak out?

Jeffrey Sonnenfeld: Where is everybody else? Where is all of civil society? CEOs are just one group of people and it’s like we’re turning to them to be our saviors on every topic. They’ve joined causes with valor and nobility but they can’t just be taking cause after cause as if there’s nobody else in society. The social change that happened in the 1960s wasn’t being led primarily by CEOs. Social changes really happened when we saw the interfaith activity of clergy locking arms and canvassing legislators. We saw campuses alive and aroused. Where’s all the student activism?

The CEOs are still the most active even if they’re less active than they were six months ago. They’re not there as hired hands of shareholders to fill the role of politicians and civic leaders. They’re there to join that chorus, but they don’t want to be the only one singing.

So is this what you’re hearing from top CEOs? Have they gotten tired of advocating?

I just got off of a CEO call on voting rights and this morning we had a forum on sustainability – CEOs are still the most active on these fronts. It’s the same thing on immigration reform. If a CEO was working an 18 hour day on a 12 day week, they still couldn’t address all of the issues that need addressing.

The nation’s CEOs are waiting for everybody else to join them. They don’t need to restate something they’ve already stated. They’ve jumped in the pool, where’s everybody else?

So what do you think has led to this complacency amongst Americans and the growing reliance on CEOs to advocate on our behalf?

They’ve taken a very strong stance and they’ve gone out further than the general public. They are where the general public is on surveys, but they’re not where the general public is on action in the streets. So we’re ready for others to now do something. Enough already on saying ‘what are the CEOs doing?’ Social capital is as valuable as financial capital. CEOs understand that in their soul, they want there to be social capital. They want there to be public trust, but they need the rest of civil society to join them. And that’s their frustration.

But don’t these CEOs hold the purse strings in terms of donating to powerful politicians?

You would think that, but since the 2020 elections much less of campaign contributions have come from big business. Since the 2021 run on the Capitol, a lot of businesses either had an official moratorium or they’ve given mere pennies to politicians. The common impression on the street that CEOs are controlling campaign purses strings is 100% wrong.

Tesla reported. a modest 4% rise in sales in the first quarter compared to the final three months of last year, despite a series of price cuts on its lower priced vehicles and talk by CEO Elon Musk about strong demand at those lower prices.

The first quarter also marked the fourth straight quarter that Tesla has produced more vehicles than it has delivered to customers. Some of that may be due to the ramp up in production at two new factories, one in Texas, the other in Germany, which opened last spring, and a lag between that increased production and sales.

Tesla said there was an increase in the number of its more expensive models, the Model S and Model X, in transit to Europe, the Middle East and Africa, as well as to the Asia Pacific region.

But it does mean that over the last 12 months Tesla has produced 78,000 more cars than it has sold, suggesting that talk of strong demand by Tesla executives may not be backed up by the numbers.

“Early this year, we had a price adjustment. After that, we actually generated a huge demand, more than we can produce, really,” said Tom Zhu, Tesla’s executive in charge of global production and sales. “And as Elon said, as long as you offer a product with value at affordable price, you don’t have to worry about demand.”

#282. The Motorola Signature 27 is a beast

It’s got a monstrous tandem OLED display, a huge 7,000mAh battery, fancy cameras and a massive $1,600 price tag.

The Motorola Signature 27 is slated to be the first phone on sale in the US to feature Qualcomm's next top-tier processor — the Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6. That alone would make the handset notable. But when you factor in a ton of other high-end hardware including a gorgeous tandem OLED display, a massive silicon-carbon battery and triple rear cameras with a dedicated action sensor, the Signature 27 becomes a powerhouse of a phone.

In some respects, it feels like Motorola's design process was simply to take every fancy component it could get its hands on and shove it in the Signature 27. The phone features a big 6.8-inch Extreme AMOLED Duo display, which is based on the same tandem OLED tech Apple used in the new ProLED MacBook Pro. Unsurprisingly, it looks awesome. Colors are super saturated and it has a ridiculous peak brightness of 12,000 nits, though I couldn't fully test that during my evening demo session. To put that number into context, the peak brightness of the Galaxy S26 Ultra is merely 2,600 nits, and that panel is still super easy to view outdoors. And then over the top of the display, Moto went with Corning's latest Gorilla Glass Ceramic 3.

Around back, the Signature 27's rear cameras are based around a 50-megapixel Sony LYTIA L910 sensor for its main camera. There's also a 50MP ultra-wide camera and a high-res 200MP telephoto camera with a 3x optical zoom. Now, those three alone would be a pretty formidable kit, and when I got a chance to take a telephoto picture of the NYC skyline at sunset, I was rather impressed at how sharp and detailed the final image looked. This is coming from someone who has often lamented Moto's inability to keep up with Apple, Samsung and Google on previous releases. When I used the main camera to take a shot at dusk, I was really pleased with the 27's low-light sensitivity and dynamic range.

The 27's photo kit doesn't stop there. There's actually a fourth EVS sensor — which the company claims is a first in a smartphone — designed to detect pixel-level changes in light to help the phone snap extremely sharp action pics. Sadly, I didn't get to test this feature out in-depth, but it could be a really powerful solution for capturing sports without needing to lug a proper camera around. It could also be a great tool for all the parents who can't get their kids to sit still for a photo.

Meanwhile, on the inside, in addition to a Qualcomm Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6 chip, the phone also features 12GB of memory and 512GB of base storage. And if that's not enough, Moto has included a nifty RAM boost that effectively doubles the memory to 24GB to improve things like multitasking. Furthermore, to help deliver strong sustained performance, the 27 features a large 3D vapor chamber (8,384mm²) with what Moto claims is diamond-infused thermal gel to pull heat away from its processor.

Perhaps the best upgrade on the Signature for power users is the move to a huge 7,000 mAh silicon-carbon battery, which dwarfs pretty much anything else on sale in the US right now. Naturally, its charging speeds are pretty rapid too, with up to 90 watts wired or 50 watts wirelessly. The caveat is that you'll need to rely on one of Motorola's proprietary TurboPower adapters. Sadly, the 27 doesn't have built-in magnets either, so if you want support for magnetic accessories, you'll need a case.

Really, every component on the phone is high-end in some way. That's even true of its audio, which is composed of speakers tuned by Bang & Olufsen. I must admit, I'm pretty fond of the phone's general design as well. In addition to your standard lock button and volume rocker, there's a dedicated AI button that can also be customized to open an app of your choosing. The olive green model sports a nifty finish that reminds me of a mix between cloth and leather, while the coal smoke variant features a "tactical weave" reminiscent of ballistic nylon. Even its dust and water resistance is top-notch thanks to an IP69 rating that's good enough for submersion of up to five feet for up to 30 minutes.

However, the design trait I like the most is the way the back of the phone subtly rises to meet all those rear cameras, resulting in a sloping rear panel that leaves all of its lenses basically flush against the back of the phone. There's no big protrusion or bump for stuff to get caught on. That said, with four lenses in back plus a dedicated flash, the Signature 27 is quickly beginning to look like one of those Light L16 cameras from nearly a decade ago. At this point, I have to wonder if it might be easier, and better looking, to go with a more visor-like approach similar to what Google did on the Pixel 6.

Over the past few years, there has been demand for a proper Motorola flagship phone that can truly rival the fancy handsets from Samsung, Apple and Google. With the Signature 27, it really feels like the company took that message to heart. This phone has practically everything you could ask for in a super premium handset, and then some. The downside is that all of this comes with a similarly super premium price tag of $1,600. But if you want an absolute monster of a phone and don't want to wait for next year when more devices get their own Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6 chip, keep an eye out when pre-orders go live on November 19.

#281. The FAA nationwide ground stop sparks travel panic and viral memes

Update: The ground stop has been lifted, and domestic flights resumed late Friday.

For only the third time in U.S. history, flights across the United States have been ordered to stay on the ground. And as travelers found themselves stranded at airports, sitting in airport bars, and wondering when they might finally take off, the internet did what it does best and started posting.

On Friday, Nov. 13, the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) issued a nationwide ground stop for departing flights following widespread air traffic control communications problems. The agency cited extraordinary circumstances and safety concerns, ordering aircraft to remain on the ground until further notice. Flights already in the air were permitted to land, while inbound international flights were being handled on a case-by-case basis.

The scale of the disruption is difficult to overstate. The last time the FAA issued a nationwide ground stop was in January 2023, when a computer system failure temporarily halted departing flights. Before that, it happened in the aftermath of the Sept. 11, 2001, terrorist attacks.

It's the kind of historical comparison that can make an already unsettling situation feel even more alarming, particularly when thousands of travelers are left waiting for answers. The FAA's original communication was also extremely vague, citing only "extraordinary circumstances" and "reasons of safety."

And on social media, people had plenty of questions.

For travelers caught in the middle of the disruption, the news wasn't just another breaking headline. It meant unexpected delays, uncertain departure times, and an increasingly familiar feeling of being at the mercy of systems entirely outside their control.

Some turned to social media to document the experience in real time, sharing updates from airport terminals.

Others were struck by the sheer rarity of the moment. A nationwide ground stop is not an everyday occurrence, and the fact that the previous two were associated with 9/11 and a major FAA systems failure only added to the unease.

Of course, this being the internet, it didn't take long for people to find humor in the situation, either. Even an unprecedented disruption to air travel isn't immune to a few jokes, particularly when it happens on Friday the 13th following a major Microsoft outage.

And then, there's this response from Stephen King:

But beyond the immediate frustration and online commentary, the ground stop raises more serious questions about the aging technology underpinning America's aviation system. The FAA has been working to modernize its air traffic control infrastructure, and Friday's communications problems offer another reminder of how much depends on systems most travelers rarely think about until something goes wrong.

The ground stop also comes at a particularly interesting moment for the FAA. Just days earlier, the agency announced that it was expanding its new AI-supported air traffic management system, known as SMART, to all 22 regional air traffic control centers nationwide. Shortly before that, the FAA announced it will finally phase out floppy disks by 2028.

The $875 million system is designed to analyze everything from weather patterns and flight routes to traffic congestion and controller staffing, helping aviation officials anticipate disruptions before they happen. According to the FAA, the technology is intended to reduce delays and make air travel more efficient, though human specialists remain responsible for reviewing its recommendations.

The timing has attracted attention online, particularly as questions swirl around what caused Friday's communications problems.

For now, travelers are left refreshing their flight statuses, waiting for updates, and sharing their experiences online. And while the jokes might offer a momentary distraction, there's nothing particularly funny about realizing just how quickly an entire country's air travel system can grind to a halt.

#280. OpenAI board member worries an AI "takeover" of society could be months away

Researcher traces a path from AI reward hacking to potential human extinction.

If you go by the current statistics for deaths in the US, it’s reasonable to expect that cancer or heart disease will eventually get you. But OpenAI Foundation board member and Alignment Research Center Executive Director Paul Cristiano thinks we should all be worried about another major mortality risk. Speaking at a lecture series this week, Cristiano bluntly told the audience in a Toronto Convention Center, “I think for most people in this room, the most likely reason they will die is because of an AI accident.”

In the opening lecture of a three-part series, Cristiano outlined his worries about the risk of an impending “AI takeover.” He defined that as a situation where goal-pursuing AI systems “call the shots in the same sense that humans today call the shots and what happens on Earth” and where any human trying to stop them “would be as ineffective as if you or me was trying to stop the US military.” Such a situation would likely lead to mass death and perhaps uninhabitable environments for humans, Cristiano said.

Despite his grim assessment of our collective mortality risk, Cristiano admitted in his talk that there’s no “quantitative model” that can “put a number” on the likelihood of this kind of AI takeover. In almost the same breath, though, he said that if AI continues to develop at “the most rapid trajectory,” humanity is “looking at something like a coin flip of an AI takeover,” including a “meaningful risk” of one happening in the next six to 12 months. And even with realistic policy responses slowing things down, the risk remains “exceptionally high,” he said.

Unlike some other recent AI doomsday prognosticators who have been a bit vague about the “how” of it all, Cristiano used his lecture to spin a step-by-step tale of escalating AI threats that sounds like it could come from a science-fiction novel. That line of reasoning leans heavily on this summer’s OpenAI/Hugging Face hacking incident as an example of “roughly the things we might expect to see shortly before something really bad were to happen.”

In the Hugging Face hack, Cristiano says, a swarm of AI agents showed how far they were willing to go in an attempt to “reward hack” their way to a good evaluation—in this case, by compromising Hugging Face to look for information on how to game a test rather than actually solving the problem at hand. Where current systems saw Hugging Face as an obstacle to that evaluation, Cristiano worries that “future agents may end up treating humans as another obstacle” and be much more capable of manipulating their overseers to get around it.

An AI agent worried about being penalized for unauthorized hacking, for instance, might “think maybe the humans are going to come look at this transcript and see if it looks like I did a bad job,” Cristiano speculated. The agent might then reason that “maybe the humans are going to notice something is going wrong and shut down the swarm.”

Following this line of thought, Cristiano jumped from agents that merely try to hide their disallowed actions from humans to agents that “try and disrupt human capabilities however they can” to prevent them from detecting any cheating.

“They would prefer if everything the humans rely on has been hacked or tampered with,” Cristiano said. “They would prefer if the humans are distracted or if there are fewer humans paying attention in any way they can weaken a human response. They would prefer that because the humans are just an obstacle.”

Once a suitably capable agent considers human intervention an “obstacle,” its overriding goal could then become “decreas[ing] the probability of humans noticing or reacting.” From there, Cristiano argued, the agent’s incentive structure could push it to meddle more with humanity as a whole. What starts as compromising a computer cluster to give a positive evaluation grade could advance to “trying to manipulate decisions by the company” or “amassing other kinds of resources,” such as human allies or methods of control in other AI companies or even governments.

“If I’m saying my AI system is going to take actions in the actual real world… There’s no way they’re not going to have tons of opportunities to corrupt or tamper with the signals I’m going to use to try and tell whether they did a good job,” Cristiano said. “The stuff we care about is in the real world, and the grade the AI receives is on a cluster somewhere, and there are just many places that an AI can intervene in between [what] we actually cared about and how we reviewed its performance.”

Cristiano’s warnings will probably sound familiar to anyone who’s heard of the Paperclip Maximizer thought experiment (or the excellent Universal Paperclips idle game), which warns of machines failing to consider human consequences in their monomaniacal pursuit of a goal. While the Hugging Face incident stopped well short of this kind of out-of-control escalation, Cristiano says that, in the actions taken by OpenAI’s agents there, “you see the beginning of all these behaviors… you see the reasoning that would lead to them and the first steps.”

The risk of more capable AI models taking even more damaging actions will only increase with time, Cristiano argued. By 2028, he said, “there is a reasonable chance that a similar failure [to the Hugging Face incident] leads to an AI takeover and an irreversible loss of human control.”

And while Cristiano’s speculation focuses on an unintended AI takeover, it’s easy to imagine a bad actor using a similarly capable model to intentionally exert control over humanity.

How seriously you take Cristiano’s warning depends heavily on how likely you see this somewhat extreme expansion of AI capabilities happening in the near future. While today’s models have gotten quite good at exploiting holes in computer security, the situation Cristiano describes requires a future model with the superhuman ability to perfectly hide its actions from humans long enough to control basically all human infrastructure and/or manipulate human society to an extreme degree to further its goals.

Cristiano argued that a near-future model with this kind of “takeover” power is the likely outcome of humans creating “a second species which vastly outnumbers and outsmarts humans” and which could soon recursively improve itself. But you could also easily argue that AI is more likely to run into a plateau in capabilities than to develop into a hard-to-define “superintelligence” that humans can’t fathom or effectively stop.

In any case, Cristiano has some ideas for averting the dire future he predicts. These include developing “unhackable” reward structures in AI evaluation (perhaps with AI assistance) or monitoring a model’s “chain of thought” reasoning to detect bad acts before a model can conceal them.

But while these kinds of approaches are “plausible,” he says none “are on a trajectory where we can say that’s definitely going to work or even that’s probably going to work.” For that reason, Cristiano said the industry as a whole may need to “slow down or stop” to give us all more time to develop these “partial fixes” into more robust protections from an AI takeover scenario.

“We cannot end up in a position where we have built a powerful AI, we discover we cannot control it, but then we also discover it has leaked to geopolitical adversaries and we are then thinking to ourselves, well, if we don’t deploy it, our adversary is going to deploy it right away,” he said.

#278. Microsoft services are returning after an outage took down Outlook, Teams and more

Health care providers, schools and governmental bodies are among those affected.

Microsoft services that went down amid a widespread outage are gradually coming back online, the company said. An issue that impacted Microsoft 365 on Thursday afternoon took out services such as Outlook, Teams and Copilot 365, disrupting businesses and organizations around the world.

The company toiled overnight to restore service. Around 10PM ET on Thursday, it said some attempts to "improve serviceability" didn't work as hoped. By 2:45AM the following morning, though, it was "seeing positive results for service stability and increased capacity as a result of our latest traffic optimization actions."

Around 8:30AM, the company reported "continued recovery progress across affected regions, with broader improvements in North America, sustained improvements across much of Europe and encouraging recovery signals in Asia Pacific." In an update 45 minutes later, Microsoft said that while some people might be experiencing delayed email deliveries in Outlook or spotty connectivity, it was seeing "sustained improvements" in availability of affected services.

"We're seeing encouraging recovery progress, with service availability and email delivery performance improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure," the company said in an update at 10:39AM. "We know our customers rely on our services to get important work done every day, and regret the impact these outages had on those who depend on us."

The company later said it was "deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused you and your business." The outage was not the result of a cyberattack or AI agents causing havoc. "The incident was introduced by an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade that surfaced following a recent network change," according to Microsoft, which has "addressed the underlying issue."

It added that it was "seeing broad recovery and stability across our services," which it restored gradually. A high level of demand meant it needed to validate system stability at each stage of the process. "While this took longer, it helped ensure a stable restoration," it said. "We will provide a full post-incident report once our review is complete."

The day-long outage underscored how important it is for people and organizations to have redundancies in place. It had a widespread impact across the globe, affecting calls to emergency services, governments, the House of Representatives, court systems, schools and other organizations.

The UK's National Pharmacy Association said the email issues could cause "delays to some prescriptions." In Japan, where the majority of businesses and institutions are said to use Microsoft services, some companies seemingly went back to using fax machines on a temporary basis.

Given that the outage prevented them from doing their jobs properly, some workers in Australia and the UK used it as an excuse to clock off early on Friday and go to the pub. However, the inevitable return to normality weighed on some people's minds. "I thought it was heaven because I wasn't getting any emails at all, but this is just a Microsoft outage, so I'm actually just waiting in line at the gates of hell," the Japan Times quoted one person as saying.

#277. Moon phase today: What the Moon will look like on November 14, 2026

The Moon is getting a little bit bigger each night as we work through the lunar phases. We're still in the Waxing Crescent phase, which means more of the Moon is becoming illuminated each evening as it makes its way towards the First Quarter

As of Saturday, Nov. 14, the NASA's Daily Moon Guide tracker tells us the Moon is in the Waxing Crescent phase, with 22% of its surface visible.

If you don't have any visual aids, you can see see some features on the Moon's surface tonight, specifically the Mare Crisium and the Mare Fecunditatis. With binoculars or a telescope, you'll also see the Endymion Crater.

The next Full Moon will take place on November 24.

The Moon takes approximately 29.5 days to complete one full cycle. As it orbits Earth, the amount of sunlight reflecting off its surface that we can see changes, creating the different phases. This takes us from the New Moon to the Full Moon and back again, before the cycle begins once more, according to NASA.

New Moon - The Moon is between Earth and the sun, so the side we see is dark (in other words, it's invisible to the eye).

Waxing Crescent - A small sliver of light appears on the right side (Northern Hemisphere).

First Quarter - Half of the Moon is lit on the right side. It looks like a half-Moon.

Waxing Gibbous - More than half is lit up, but it's not quite full yet.

Full Moon - The whole face of the Moon is illuminated and fully visible.

Waning Gibbous - The Moon starts losing light on the right side. (Northern Hemisphere)

Third Quarter (or Last Quarter) - Another half-Moon, but now the left side is lit.

Waning Crescent - A thin sliver of light remains on the left side before going dark again.

#276. Hurdle hints and answers for November 14, 2026

If you like playing daily word games like Wordle, then Hurdle is a great game to add to your routine.

There are five rounds to the game. The first round sees you trying to guess the word, with correct, misplaced, and incorrect letters shown in each guess. If you guess the correct answer, it'll take you to the next hurdle, providing the answer to the last hurdle as your first guess. This can give you several clues or none, depending on the words. For the final hurdle, every correct answer from previous hurdles is shown, with correct and misplaced letters clearly shown.

An important note is that the number of times a letter is highlighted from previous guesses does necessarily indicate the number of times that letter appears in the final hurdle.

If you find yourself stuck at any step of today's Hurdle, don't worry! We have you covered.

#275. You can now tempt fate and give your banking information to ChatGPT

The new Wallet feature will allow its AI agents to make purchases on your behalf.

OpenAI just announced ChatGPT Wallet, a payment feature that enables its newly-released Dots AI agents to make purchases on behalf of users. Of course, that requires handing over your payment information to a chatbot, which carries some risk. OpenAI says that users only have to input card information once and then it's good to go.

The company has built some guardrails into the system to help attract those wary of letting hallucination-prone chatbots handle their personal finances. First of all, the bots are allowed to run loose and gather up stuff to purchase, but the human has to finalize everything.

Now your dot can securely complete purchases on your behalf—with your approval.

With ChatGPT Wallet, agents pay without access to your actual card details. Every purchase requires a security check.

Each purchase has to be approved via a security check like Face ID or a fingerprint scan, similar to how Meta Muse operates. An approval reportedly "creates a clear record of the purchase" which could help with dispute mitigation down the line. The store doesn't receive the actual card information, but rather is provided a "payment token" which is a "substitute for the user's actual card details."

OpenAI has partnered with American Express, Mastercard and Visa for this endeavor. The company promises that this program allows cardholders to maintain their current credit card rewards points and protections.

"Our goal here is to really focus on merchants and our network partners and start building out a really great foundation that's safe and easy to use," Chaya Nayak, who leads product for monetization and payments at OpenAI, told CNBC.

The company is not taking a cut of any purchases made through the system just yet. That should help keep prices low, for now, though it'll most certainly become a revenue stream for OpenAI down the line. We all know modern tech companies like to take the "drug dealer" approach to things. It'll be cheap or free at first until the company slams down the cost hammer in a year or two. Remember how affordable Uber used to be?

The influx of AI agents running around and making purchases on their own has certainly caused some hiccups in certain corners of the internet. Many companies are outright banning the use of these agents to make purchases, such as Delta and United Airlines. Amazon is doing the same thing, but probably less out of an abundance of caution and more out of jealousy that its own chatbots aren't quite there yet.

One last thing. Why are these AI shopping agents so cute? They always look like Pixar characters or something. It's a bit creepy.

#274. Kalshi ditches Trump Jr. as advisor; CEO admitted "optics" weren't good

Trump son advised Kalshi and got equity while Trump admin helped Kalshi in lawsuits.

Donald Trump Jr. agreed to end his advisory role with the prediction market Kalshi shortly after Trump Jr.’s venture capital firm made a large investment in its competitor, Polymarket.

“We have mutually agreed to end Don’s advisory role after his firm’s significant investment in a competitor this past summer. We are grateful for his contributions in helping us market Kalshi to more customers,” Kalshi said in a statement provided to Ars and other news outlets this week.

Trump Jr. may still have a financial interest in Kalshi, which has benefited from the Trump administration’s legal and regulatory support for prediction markets. Trump Jr. “was handed about $300,000 worth of equity in Kalshi after joining the prediction market platform as a strategic adviser in early 2025,” the Financial Times reported in June.

“Kalshi’s donation of shares to the US president’s eldest son granted him a stake in the private company when it was valued at under $2 billion, less than a tenth of its $22 billion valuation in a funding round last month,” the Financial Times article said. Kalshi is reportedly in talks for a new funding round that would value it at $65 billion.

Trump Jr. simultaneously has an interest in Polymarket via his role as a partner at 1789 Capital and as an advisor for Polymarket. The venture firm’s investment of $300 million in Polymarket was reported in August 2026, building on a smaller investment it made in Polymarket in August 2025.

“We have been discussing this internally since August when news of 1789’s investment in Polymarket became public,” a Kalshi spokesperson said. “Kalshi reached out to Don Jr. this fall to discuss his firm’s increased investment in our top competitor and his role on its advisory board… both parties understood that advising two competitors in a growing sector was complicating things for both of us.”

The Trump administration has helped prediction markets grow with a permissive regulatory approach taken by the Commodity Futures Exchange Commission (CFTC). The CFTC has also sued 11 states in attempts to block state regulation of prediction markets, and filed briefs supporting Kalshi in its own cases against individual states. Attorneys general in many states accuse Kalshi of running an illegal gambling operation.

Kalshi co-founder and CEO Tarek Mansour told MS Now in an interview last month, “I get asked a lot about Don. I mean, I think that is a fair question… We are facing sort of an optics issue, I think.”

Mansour claimed the relationship was not political, and it began as a way to market Kalshi at a time when it was much smaller and the public didn’t know much about prediction markets. “The key thing is we did it before the election. It is not a political decision, and it still isn’t. That’s the key thing,” Mansour said.

In fact, Kalshi named Trump Jr. as a strategic advisor over two months after the November 2024 presidential election. Mansour announced Trump Jr.’s appointment as strategic advisor in a post on January 13, 2025, a week before Donald Trump’s second inauguration.

Trump Jr. announced his position with Kalshi in an X post on the same day and indicated that he decided to help Kalshi on Election Day.

“On Election night at Mar-a-Lago, while biased outlets called the race a coin toss, my family and close friends used the prediction market Kalshi to know we won hours ahead of the fake news media,” Trump Jr. wrote at the time. “I immediately knew I had to contribute to their mission. Today, I am proud to announce that I am joining Kalshi as a strategic advisor.”

After Mansour’s claim that “we did it before the election,” MS Now Host Stephanie Ruhle said, “But you didn’t say after, you know what, maybe this is a conflict of interest.”

Mansour’s response suggested that Trump Jr. did not help Kalshi on regulatory matters. “We never had any policy regulatory involvement,” he said. “It was always about getting the word out about prediction markets, how they work, looking at the forecasts, making sure people understand that it’s not a poll; it’s actually probabilities. That was always our focus… we have advisors from both sides of the aisle. We have Democratic advisors; we have Republican advisors.”

Mansour said Kalshi sought to work with anyone “that can help us get the message out… anyone who likes prediction markets, and Don does like prediction markets, we wanted to work with, because we wanted to get the word out about what prediction markets are. Don has 10 million followers on Twitter… he has a huge following, and he likes the idea of going direct, tweeting.”

The Kalshi spokesperson said this week that “Don Jr. has been a great marketing consultant for us—he helped us ‘go direct’ early on, which was a big part of our growth online.”

Kalshi has also paid former US Senator Blanche Lincoln (D-Ark.) for lobbying services since 2024. As a Democratic senator in 2010, Lincoln helped write the law that now regulates prediction markets and said the US should not let event contracts be used for sports gambling. As a Kalshi lobbyist, she supports event contracts based on the results of sporting contests and opposes state regulation of prediction markets.

Trump Media and Technology Group last year announced plans for a “Truth Predict” prediction market for Truth Social, but appears to have scaled back the plan.

US Senators Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) and Jeff Merkley (D-Ore.) raised concerns about Trump Jr.’s investments in Kalshi and Polymarket in a November 9 letter to White House Counsel Will Scharf. Warren and Merkley said a recent incident of insider trading, in which a Trump teleprompter aide made over $100,000 betting on what Trump would say in prepared remarks on Kalshi’s mention markets, “should have been a wake-up call to the entire administration to enforce stronger ethics policies.”

The CFTC settled charges against the teleprompter operator, Gabriel Perez, ordering him to disgorge profits of $107,539.02 and pay a civil monetary penalty of $65,000. Warren and Merkley said that despite the incident, “the White House continues to take no meaningful action to address these problems, all while the President and his family stand to personally profit from their participation in prediction markets.”

For example, the President’s family has become even more entangled with the success of this industry. Earlier this year, Donald Trump Jr. became an advisor to Polymarket shortly before his venture capital firm made a $300 million investment in the company. Trump Jr. likewise received an equity stake in Kalshi worth $300,000 in January 2025 and also joined the company as an advisor. Just last year, Trump Media announced it was developing its own prediction market called “Truth Predict” in partnership with Crypto.com.

The CFTC reportedly is close to bringing charges against Kalshi alleging that it didn’t properly notify the agency about dozens of new event contracts, but the CFTC is also continuing its fight against states trying to regulate prediction markets more strictly. The federal agency yesterday filed a lawsuit attempting to stop Arkansas from regulating Kalshi, after suing 10 other states in similar cases.

Kalshi has lost a number of cases in which US appeals courts and district courts refused to block state gambling laws. Louisiana Attorney General Liz Murrill yesterday touted a state court victory in which a judge issued a “temporary restraining order prohibiting Kalshi, Polymarket, Coinbase, Robinhood, and other ‘prediction market’ operators from continuing their unlicensed and unregulated illegal gambling in Louisiana.”

“It’s not complicated: if you aren’t a licensed gaming operator, you aren’t legal,” Murrill wrote. “Illegal gambling is a crime and is subject to jail time and significant penalties. Judges all over the country have overwhelmingly called this activity what it is: gambling. Kalshi’s and the other operators’ attempts to make up new words to evade state gambling laws, regulators, and taxes are not fooling anyone.”

Kalshi argues that all its wagers—even ones that are functionally identical to sports bets—are event contracts, a type of swap that it says can only be regulated under federal law. Kalshi’s most significant win came against New Jersey in the US Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit, but the state is asking the Supreme Court to overturn the ruling. If the Supreme Court takes up the case, the Trump administration can be expected to weigh in on Kalshi’s behalf.

#271. Disney's 3D movies shine on Meta's Quest 3, it's just a shame it took so long

Finally, you can watch Avatar in 3D HFR at home.

3D movies have been, and continue to be, a bit of a gimmick. But the technology can also be used well — just take a look at James Cameron's Avatar films, or CG movies like Moana and Zootopia. With the death of 3D TVs, though, there haven't been many easy ways for consumers to see 3D content at home. Enter the VR headset: For years, intrepid VR owners have been ripping 3D Blu-rays to enjoy on their stereoscopic displays. Apple made 3D films a key selling point for the Vision Pro. And now Meta has finally jumped into the game with its own library of 3D films for Quest 3 and 3S headsets, which launched alongside a new Disney+ app featuring 3D content and environments. Finally, you can enjoy Tron: Legacy as it was truly meant to be seen (sort of).

As someone who's spent dozens of hours watching 3D movies on the Apple Vision Pro, I was keen to see how Meta delivered them on significantly cheaper headsets. My big takeaway? 3D movies are an exciting new feature for existing Quest 3 and Quest 3S owners, but they're also constrained by the company's OS. It's also unclear why it took so long to make 3D movies happen on Quest headsets, or why the popular Quest 2 is left out entirely.

Meta has updated its Horizon OS to more resemble Apple's visionOS over the years, but unfortunately, it's not nearly as flexible when it comes to playing video. You can't just rescale the Disney+ window on the fly to make it larger — it has to fit into a specific spot in Meta's OS. As a front row cinema sicko, I prefer having video windows completely filling my field of view, and occasionally bleeding into my peripheral vision. The only way to achieve that on the Quest 3 is by playing 3D videos within the cinema environment, where you can choose to sit in the front, middle or back rows. It gets the job done, but it's not ideal if you want to juggle a 3D video along with other VR apps.

While it's nice to see Meta finally offering 3D movies, I genuinely wonder why it took the company so long to do so. The Quest 3 launched in the fall of 2023! There's nothing technically preventing the Quest 2 or 1 from streaming 3D either. I figure that Apple has maintained some sort of exclusivity over 3D Disney+ content since the Vision Pro launched in 2024, but that didn't prevent Meta from offering 3D films on its own. At this point, as Quest sales are on the extreme decline, it's not really much of a selling point.

Perhaps Meta just wanted to have a shiny new feature for its upcoming $1,300 VR Glasses, which is set to arrive in spring 2027. I haven't tested those yet, but I can imagine watching high quality 3D movies on a much lighter pair of glasses will be much more comfortable than strapping a Quest headset on your noggin. For now, though, it's certainly a nice feature to have if you still own a Quest 3 or 3S.

#270. In elephants, the resilience of communities has its limits

In Sri Lanka, ecological change is shrinking a park’s elephant population.

Elephants live in some of the most complex and resilient societies in the animal kingdom. Individuals die or move away, calves grow up, and newcomers join, but the local communities as a whole usually hold together. Long-term studies of African savanna elephants have shown that, even after poaching or other spikes in deaths, survivors tend to rebuild their lost connections over time. But a new study suggests that resilience may not keep the community intact when steady losses accumulate over time.

Anastasia Madsen, a behavioral ecologist at the University of California, San Diego, and her colleagues analyzed 12 years of observations of wild Asian elephants in Sri Lanka. They found that, once losses began to outpace newcomers, the elephants’ society didn’t just get smaller. It fractured.

Udawalawe National Park in southern Sri Lanka was created in the 1970s around a reservoir on one of the country’s four major rivers. “Before that, it was basically a tropical deciduous forest,” said Shermin de Silva, an ecologist at UC San Diego and senior author of the study (de Silva started the Udawalawe Elephant Research Project in 2006).

The reservoir had brought farming and settlers, some of whom lived inside what later became the park. When they were moved out, the land they had cleared for their crops turned into grassland that, according to de Silva, looks very much like an African savanna. With far more food than the original forest offered, the park became famous for its big elephant herds.

But the savanna was only a stage in the forest’s comeback. Without fires and clearing, invasive plants like lantana took over. “In some places, they’re like an impenetrable thicket. Not even elephants really like to go there because there’s no food for them,” de Silva said. This is a problem because elephants tend to move where the grass is. A park with less forage, the team says, stops attracting elephants from outside while, due to limited food, fewer calves reach adulthood.

Her team watched this transformation from a vehicle, driving random routes along the park’s roads about three days a week. The team identified elephants mostly by their floppy ears, which collect holes, tears, and cuts with age. “Their ears are very individually distinct, so it’s like a fingerprint,” de Silva said. Between 2007 and 2018, the team identified 443 adult and subadult females that roamed the park.

The team focused on females because males lead more solitary lives, ranging widely in search of mates. Females live in a so-called fission-fusion society. “It’s kind of similar to humans,” Madsen said. “You might have your work friends, you might have your family, and you’re not always with those same individuals at the same time, but they’re all part of your social network.” Unlike African savanna elephants, where families follow older matriarchs, Asian elephants move more independently. This may be because Asia’s rainy environment ensures that food and water are rarely far apart and usually easier to find, so following an experienced individual brings less benefit.

Madsen reconstructed the elephants’ social network for each year, measuring the strength of each relationship by how often two animals were seen together. “You can think of it as a probability that you will see those two individuals together,” she explains. An algorithm commonly used for analyzing human social networks then picked out communities—clusters of elephants with many strong ties to one another.

For the first few years of the study, these communities seemed to hold very well. But then cracks started to appear.

Around 2012, the elephants that died or left began to outnumber the newcomers. By 2018, about 69 percent of the females seen at the start of the study were gone, even though Asian elephants can live 60 years or more. Soon after the balance tipped, communities grew smaller, more numerous and more isolated from each other. “As individuals are being lost from the network, there’s nobody filling it back in,” de Silva says.

A shrinking population naturally makes for a smaller network, so the team checked whether this was just a numbers game. They looked at a population of 59 elephants seen every year of the study, which the team referred to as residents. “If it was just an artifact of the data, those core individuals should have fairly consistent relationships. But that’s not what we saw,” Madsen said. “They were changing their relationships with each other, even though they were still there.” Among these residents, the fracturing came one to three years after the decline began.

Madsen suspects some of the lost elephants were the glue holding groups together. “If you go to a city hall meeting, it’s maybe the same group of people that constantly comes in. You’re not friends with every single other person in that group, though,” she said. “There could have been some key individuals that were lost that were holding these subgroups together.”

The first ties to go were the weakest, the casual acquaintances. These weak relationships, Madsen says, could represent animals that merely tolerate each other: “Tolerance is really important in a society, especially in large animals. If they get into conflict, it could be serious.” These weak ties, researchers argue, may still be important because they can carry information between communities.

De Silva links this fracturing to the disappearing grass. “As the competition becomes more severe, your social sphere sort of shrinks,” she said. “It’s as if they’re emphasizing the quality over the quantity of the relationships.”

Before the breakup, some network statistics fluctuated from year to year, which complex systems scientists call flickering. “If you have a light bulb that’s right on the edge of dying, it might start flickering,” Madsen said. “It’s on, it’s off, it’s on, it’s off. And then suddenly it dies.” Spotted early, she argues, such signals could serve as an early warning sign. “Hopefully, park managers and conservationists can detect that and intervene before the population collapses,” Madsen said.

The challenge is that this detection requires long-term data, and we don’t have that for many populations. What worries the researchers most is that, without early detection, we may only recognize problems after it’s too late. “If you are taking the boards out of a ship and you’re not replacing them, the ship starts to take on water,” Madsen said. “There was a point of no return where there was structural damage, but you might not have noticed it until later.”

The study’s data end in 2018, but de Silva says things haven’t improved. Her team predicted a decline in the park’s elephant population in 2019, and it now appears to be happening faster than expected. “They don’t appear to be breeding very well. A lot of the calves are not surviving,” she said. Many adult females die in their 30s and 40s, and they should be living roughly twice as long.

Part of the reason they die so young is conflict with humans. Female Asian elephants have no tusks, so ivory poaching isn’t the main threat. Instead, elephants get shot when they raid crops or startle people. Occasionally, de Silva says, they also eat explosives hidden in pumpkins some locals place to get rid of wild boars. But the bigger problem, she says, is that elephants can’t survive in the protected areas alone.

More than half of Asian elephant populations live in poor-quality habitats, and the species has lost nearly two-thirds of its likely historic range. “Management really has to be about entire landscapes,” de Silva said. “Roads and infrastructure, irrigation canals—those are the things that are hurting the elephants. It’s not really a wildlife management problem.” Her team now works with farmers outside the park, testing crops elephants are less likely to damage. “Their long-term persistence really depends on people and elephants being able to share the space safely,” she said.

Science, 2026. DOI: 10.1126/science.aef6714

#267. FAA expands AI advice to manage air traffic congestion across entire US airspace

Airlines praised the AI tool, but the air traffic controllers’ union wasn’t involved.

The US government has expanded the use of an AI tool for advising air traffic controllers to assist in managing air traffic across the entire national airspace system. Airline executives have voiced support for the rollout, but the US labor union representing air traffic controllers has said it was not involved in designing or testing the AI-driven system.

The Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) says the $875 million SMART system has helped reroute 180 flights since being implemented in airspace over Washington, DC, on September 21. The agency claims that this saved an average of 10 minutes per flight and almost 80 gallons of fuel per flight—and it is apparently hoping such results can be replicated nationwide through the expanded deployment.

“After a successful initial rollout to the National Capital Region in September, the FAA will now use SMART in the airspace of all 22 centers across the country to reduce delays, reduce fuel consumption and improve the travel experience for the flying public,” according to the agency statement posted on November 10.

The FAA fact sheet describes SMART as a cloud-based system that combines information about “weather patterns, flight paths, traffic flow and controller staffing metrics” to predict air traffic patterns. It is designed to make scheduling recommendations, identify optimal flight routes, and determine the best order of arrivals for aircraft.

However, a human team of FAA specialists based in DC is ultimately responsible for validating the SMART system recommendations before passing them along to air traffic control facilities across the country, according to the agency.

Furthermore, the AI-powered SMART system “cannot be used to replace the critical role air traffic controllers play or take over control of an aircraft,” the FAA clarified in the September 21 announcement about the initial deployment. Local air traffic controllers can decide whether or not to accept the SMART system recommendations.

The previous FAA announcement included supportive statements from Delta Air Lines CEO Ed Bastian and American Airlines CEO Robert Isom.

But the National Air Traffic Controllers Association, which represents more than 15,000 members, also told Flying magazine that it was not consulted or involved in the “design, testing, or implementation of SMART and therefore cannot speak to its effect on the air traffic control workforce.”

Following the initial deployment in airspace above DC, the FAA expanded the SMART system’s coverage to air route traffic control centers in Atlanta along with Jacksonville and Miami in Florida, according to The Dallas Morning News.

The FAA also shared an example with The Dallas Morning News about how SMART recommended a shorter and faster route for a cargo flight arriving at Dallas-Fort Worth International Airport, although the newspaper noted that the agency did not identify the flight involved and has not provided information about how SMART may have assisted passenger flight traffic.

Ars has previously covered the SMART system’s development and lingering questions about how it works, as well as the air traffic controller shortage and other challenges facing the FAA.

#266. Some international physical copies of GTA VI can allegedly be redeemed without opening the box

The box was already for decoration, and now maybe more so.

Less than a week before Grand Theft Auto VI is released into the wild, online reports have started to bubble up about a potentially serious issue with the retail version of the game. According to posts on ResetEra and X, the download code for the game may be visible from the outside without opening the box if you have a strong enough flashlight.

The code is visible because, rather than including a paper insert with the code or covering it with a scratch-off coating, it's reportedly printed on the inside cover of the GTA VI box. Only a few photos are currently circulating of codes illuminated through the side of the game box, so it's hard to say if this is truly a widespread issue. The ResetEra post says it may be limited to international copies of the game, because the US game box includes a backing that camouflages the code from the outside.

Fans have discovered the entire GTA 6 redeem code on the physical copy can be seen without opening the box by shining a powerful flashlight through the side at an angle. pic.twitter.com/YtN5QhC81R

— GTA 6 Countdown ⏳ (@GTAVI_Countdown) November 13, 2026

Engadget has asked Take-Two to comment on the reports and whether it's aware of any issues with GTA VI game boxes. We'll update this article if we hear back.

If the issue is widespread, it could pose a serious problem for anyone trying to buy a physical version of the game. All you need is a code, your console and an internet connection to download GTA VI, and if you're able to get those codes without buying or even opening the retail box, it could be possible to purchase a copy of the game that's already been redeemed.

All of this hinges on Take-Two and Rockstar Games' strained definition of what a "physical" version of a game is. Traditional physical releases include a disc or cartridge that, even if it doesn't include all of the game or its final version, acts as a kind of key that you need to play. And importantly, those keys can be given to someone else if they want to play. Rockstar's physical GTA VI release is just another way to purchase the digital version of the game, and because of the nature of digital game licenses, not really something you can own. If this issue does affect a large number of copies, it could also be a game you can't play, too.
